Gene 1 when dominant expresses an enzyme that creates the teal pigment from the blue precursor pigment. Gene 2 when dominant expresses an enzyme that creates the green pigment from the blue precursor pigment. When both genes are dominant, the two enzymes pathways combine to form the new white pigment. MC	<p>250e_949b</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 1</td> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: tomato">red</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: yellow">yellow</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: snow">white</td></tr></table><br/>A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color. When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the red precursor pigment into the yellow pigment. When gene 1 is homozygous recessive, there is no pigment change and the red precursor pigment remains. Gene 2 only has an effect on the pigment when gene 1 is dominant. When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the yellow pigment into the white pigment. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show recessive epistasis and a <b>9:3:4 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>8d82_b014</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 1</td> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: ivory">white</td></tr></table><br/>A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color. When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the blue precursor pigment into a a second molecule with the same blue pigment. When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the second blue pigment molecule from gene 1 into the white pigment. The white pigment forms only when both genes are dominant, if either gene is homozygous recessive, the blue pigment will remain. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate recessive epistasis and a <b>9:7 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; When both genes are dominant, the two enzymes combine to form the new white pigment. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no pigment change and only the red precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show recessive epistasis and a <b>9:3:4 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>ae44_05e3</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="3"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td></tr><tr><td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td></tr><tr><td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: ivory">white</td></tr></table><br/>When gene 2 is dominant and gene 1 is homozygous recessive, gene 2 expresses an active enzyme that converts the blue precursor pigment into the white pigment. Whenever gene 1 is dominant, then gene 1 expresses a protein that completely suppresses the activity of gene 2. When both genes are dominant, the inhibited gene 2 is unable to produce the white pigment, so the blue precursor pigment remains. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no active enzyme and again only the blue precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate recessive epistasis and a <b>9:7 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>b58d_2c51</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="2"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td><td rowspan="2"></td></tr><tr> <td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: snow">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: tomato">red</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: yellow">yellow</td></tr></table><br/> A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color.<li> <ul>When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that inhibits another enzyme that converts the white precursor pigment into the red pigment.</ul> <ul>When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the red pigment into the yellow pigment.</ul> <ul>When both genes are dominant, the dominant allele of gene 1 inhibits the production of the red color, so it will remain the white precursor color.</ul> <ul>When both of the genes are homozygous recessive, then the first enzyme is active (not inhibited) and the white precursor pigment is converted to the red pigment and it will remain the final pigment, as the second enzyme is inactive.</ul></li> <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate repeated epistasis and a <b>10:3:3 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the blue precursor pigment into a a second molecule with the same blue pigment. When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the second blue pigment molecule from gene 1 into the white pigment. The white pigment forms only when both genes are dominant, if either gene is homozygous recessive, the blue pigment will remain. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate dominant epistasis and a <b>15:1 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>fc70_8640</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="3"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td></tr><tr><td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td></tr><tr><td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: azure">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td></tr></table><br/>When gene 2 is dominant and gene 1 is homozygous recessive, gene 2 expresses an active enzyme that converts the white precursor pigment into the blue pigment. Whenever gene 1 is dominant, then gene 1 expresses a protein that completely suppresses the activity of gene 2. When both genes are dominant, the inhibited gene 2 is unable to produce the blue pigment, so the white precursor pigment remains. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no active enzyme and again only the white precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate dominant epistasis and a <b>15:1 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>805e_1397</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 1</td> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: aquamarine">teal</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: azure">white</td></tr></table><br/>A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color. When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the blue precursor pigment into the teal pigment. When gene 1 is homozygous recessive, there is no pigment change and the blue precursor pigment remains. Gene 2 only has an effect on the pigment when gene 1 is dominant. When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the teal pigment into the white pigment. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate interaction epistasis and a <b>9:6:1 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>69e0_15f3</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="2"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td><td rowspan="2"></td></tr><tr> <td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: ivory">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: palegreen">green</td></tr></table><br/> A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color.<li> <ul>When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that inhibits another enzyme that converts the blue precursor pigment into the white pigment.</ul> <ul>When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the white pigment into the green pigment.</ul> <ul>When both genes are dominant, the dominant allele of gene 1 inhibits the production of the white color, so it will remain the blue precursor color.</ul> <ul>When both of the genes are homozygous recessive, then the first enzyme is active (not inhibited) and the blue precursor pigment is converted to the white pigment and it will remain the final pigment, as the second enzyme is inactive.</ul></li> <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show recessive epistasis and a <b>9:3:4 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>895b_347a</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 1</td> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: gainsboro">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: gainsboro">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td></tr></table><br/>A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color. When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the white precursor pigment into a a second molecule with the same white pigment. When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the second white pigment molecule from gene 1 into the blue pigment. The blue pigment forms only when both genes are dominant, if either gene is homozygous recessive, the white pigment will remain. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show double duplicate epistasis and a <b>10:6 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>0bca_8138</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="3"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td></tr><tr><td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td></tr><tr><td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: sienna">brown</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: yellow">yellow</td></tr></table><br/>When gene 2 is dominant and gene 1 is homozygous recessive, gene 2 expresses an active enzyme that converts the brown precursor pigment into the yellow pigment. Whenever gene 1 is dominant, then gene 1 expresses a protein that completely suppresses the activity of gene 2. When both genes are dominant, the inhibited gene 2 is unable to produce the yellow pigment, so the brown precursor pigment remains. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no active enzyme and again only the brown precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate recessive epistasis and a <b>9:7 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; Gene 1 when dominant expresses an enzyme that creates the sky blue pigment from the blue precursor pigment. Gene 2 when dominant expresses an enzyme that creates the tan pigment from the blue precursor pigment. When both genes are dominant, the two enzymes pathways combine to form the new white pigment. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no pigment change and the blue precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show digenic inheritance and a <b>9:3:3:1 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the white precursor pigment into the tan pigment. When gene 1 is homozygous recessive, there is no pigment change and the white precursor pigment remains. Gene 2 only has an effect on the pigment when gene 1 is dominant. MC	<p>9c8a_6b70</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="2"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td><td rowspan="2"></td></tr><tr> <td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: tomato">red</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: snow">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: plum">purple</td></tr></table><br/> A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color.<li> <ul>When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that inhibits another enzyme that converts the red precursor pigment into the white pigment.</ul> <ul>When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the white pigment into the purple pigment.</ul> <ul>When both genes are dominant, the dominant allele of gene 1 inhibits the production of the white color, so it will remain the red precursor color.</ul> <ul>When both of the genes are homozygous recessive, then the first enzyme is active (not inhibited) and the red precursor pigment is converted to the white pigment and it will remain the final pigment, as the second enzyme is inactive.</ul></li> <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show dominant epistasis and a <b>12:3:1 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the white precursor pigment into a a second molecule with the same white pigment. When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the second white pigment molecule from gene 1 into the red pigment. The red pigment forms only when both genes are dominant, if either gene is homozygous recessive, the white pigment will remain. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate dominant epistasis and a <b>15:1 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; When both genes are dominant, the two enzymes combine to form the new white pigment. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no pigment change and only the red precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show dominant epistasis and a <b>12:3:1 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>53d4_6c4b</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="3"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td></tr><tr><td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td></tr><tr><td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: snow">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: tomato">red</td></tr></table><br/>When gene 2 is dominant and gene 1 is homozygous recessive, gene 2 expresses an active enzyme that converts the white precursor pigment into the red pigment. Whenever gene 1 is dominant, then gene 1 expresses a protein that completely suppresses the activity of gene 2. When both genes are dominant, the inhibited gene 2 is unable to produce the red pigment, so the white precursor pigment remains. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no active enzyme and again only the white precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show dominant and recessive epistasis and a <b>13:3 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; Gene 1 when dominant expresses an enzyme that creates the yellow pigment from the red precursor pigment. Gene 2 when dominant expresses an enzyme that creates the tan pigment from the red precursor pigment. When both genes are dominant, the two enzymes pathways combine to form the new white pigment. MC	<p>9c8a_6559</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="2"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td><td rowspan="2"></td></tr><tr> <td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: tomato">red</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: snow">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: plum">purple</td></tr></table><br/> A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color.<li> <ul>When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that inhibits another enzyme that converts the red precursor pigment into the white pigment.</ul> <ul>When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the white pigment into the purple pigment.</ul> <ul>When both genes are dominant, the dominant allele of gene 1 inhibits the production of the white color, so it will remain the red precursor color.</ul> <ul>When both of the genes are homozygous recessive, then the first enzyme is active (not inhibited) and the red precursor pigment is converted to the white pigment and it will remain the final pigment, as the second enzyme is inactive.</ul></li> <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show recessive epistasis and a <b>9:3:4 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>887b_c831</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 1</td> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: tomato">red</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: tomato">red</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: snow">white</td></tr></table><br/>A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color. When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the red precursor pigment into a a second molecule with the same red pigment. When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the second red pigment molecule from gene 1 into the white pigment. The white pigment forms only when both genes are dominant, if either gene is homozygous recessive, the red pigment will remain. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate recessive epistasis and a <b>9:7 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>26a0_f76d</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="3"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td></tr><tr><td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td></tr><tr><td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: gainsboro">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td></tr></table><br/>When gene 2 is dominant and gene 1 is homozygous recessive, gene 2 expresses an active enzyme that converts the white precursor pigment into the blue pigment. Whenever gene 1 is dominant, then gene 1 expresses a protein that completely suppresses the activity of gene 2. When both genes are dominant, the inhibited gene 2 is unable to produce the blue pigment, so the white precursor pigment remains. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no active enzyme and again only the white precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show dominant and recessive epistasis and a <b>13:3 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; Gene 1 when dominant expresses an enzyme that creates the yellow pigment from the blue precursor pigment. Gene 2 when dominant expresses an enzyme that creates the redish pink pigment from the blue precursor pigment. When both genes are dominant, the two enzymes pathways combine to form the new white pigment. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no pigment change and the blue precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show second recessive epistasis and a <b>9:4:3 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>6b6c_6aa2</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 1</td> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: tomato">red</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: lightsalmon">redish pink</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: snow">white</td></tr></table><br/>A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color. When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the red precursor pigment into the redish pink pigment. When gene 1 is homozygous recessive, there is no pigment change and the red precursor pigment remains. Gene 2 only has an effect on the pigment when gene 1 is dominant. When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the redish pink pigment into the white pigment. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate repeated epistasis and a <b>10:3:3 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the second blue pigment molecule from gene 1 into the white pigment. MC	<p>c708_89ff</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="3"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td></tr><tr><td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td></tr><tr><td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: ivory">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td></tr></table><br/>When gene 2 is dominant and gene 1 is homozygous recessive, gene 2 expresses an active enzyme that converts the white precursor pigment into the blue pigment. Whenever gene 1 is dominant, then gene 1 expresses a protein that completely suppresses the activity of gene 2. When both genes are dominant, the inhibited gene 2 is unable to produce the blue pigment, so the white precursor pigment remains. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no active enzyme and again only the white precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show double duplicate epistasis and a <b>10:6 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; MC	<p>b7dd_56bd</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 1</td> <td></td> <td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: azure">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: azure">white</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td></tr></table><br/>A two step metabolic pathway determines the pigment color. When gene 1 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the white precursor pigment into a a second molecule with the same white pigment. When gene 2 is dominant, the gene expresses an enzyme that converts the second white pigment molecule from gene 1 into the blue pigment. The blue pigment forms only when both genes are dominant, if either gene is homozygous recessive, the white pigment will remain. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show duplicate dominant epistasis and a <b>15:1 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; When both genes are dominant, the two enzymes combine to form the new red pigment. MC	<p>ae44_4aca</p> <table border="0" style="border: 0px solid white; border-collapse: collapse; "><colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <colgroup width="60"></colgroup> <tr><td rowspan="3"></td><td align="center">Gene 1</td></tr><tr><td align="center"><span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span>&darr;<span style="color: red;"><b>&#10005;</b></span></td></tr><tr><td align="center">Gene 2</td></tr><tr> <td align="center" style="background-color: cornflowerblue">blue</td> <td align="center">&xrarr;</td> <td align="center" style="background-color: ivory">white</td></tr></table><br/>When gene 2 is dominant and gene 1 is homozygous recessive, gene 2 expresses an active enzyme that converts the blue precursor pigment into the white pigment. Whenever gene 1 is dominant, then gene 1 expresses a protein that completely suppresses the activity of gene 2. When both genes are dominant, the inhibited gene 2 is unable to produce the white pigment, so the blue precursor pigment remains. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no active enzyme and again only the blue precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show dominant and recessive epistasis and a <b>13:3 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black; Gene 1 when dominant expresses an enzyme that creates the tan pigment from the white precursor pigment. Gene 2 when dominant expresses an enzyme that creates the sky blue pigment from the white precursor pigment. When both genes are dominant, the two enzymes pathways combine to form the new blue pigment. If neither of the genes are dominant, then there is no pigment change and the white precursor pigment remains. <br/> <br/><p>The diagram and description above explain the interaction of two genes. <b>Determine the dihybird cross phenotypic ratio.</b></p>	<p>These two genes would show digenic inheritance and a <b>9:3:3:1 ratio</b>.</p> <table style="border: 1px solid black;
